Braided Hopf algebras and gauge transformations
Paolo Aschieri, Giovanni Landi, Chiara Pagani

Abstract
. We study infinitesimal gauge transformations of an equivariant noncommutative principal bundle as a braided Lie algebra of derivations. For this, we analyse general K K -braided Hopf and Lie algebras, for K K a (quasi)triangular Hopf algebra of symmetries, and study their representations as braided derivations. We then study Drinfeld twist deformations of braided Hopf algebras and of Lie algebras of infinitesimal gauge transformations. We give examples coming from deformations of abelian and Jordanian type. In particular we explicitly describe the braided Lie algebra of gauge transformations of the instanton bundle and of the orthogonal bundle on the quantum sphere S θ 4 S^{4}_{\theta} .



1.
Introduction

In this paper we study different notions of gauge group and
infinitesimal gauge transformations of
noncommutative principal bundles (Hopf–Galois extensions).
This is a key ingredient for a differential
geometry study of the theory of connections and of their moduli
spaces.
The group of gauge transformations was considered in
[
11
]

(see also
[
12
]
and
[
14
]
).
A general feature of these works is that the group
there defined is bigger than one would expect. In
[
6
]
we
studied the gauge group of noncommutative bundles with commutative
base space algebra and noncommutative Hopf algebra as structure group.
In particular, we proved that noncommutative principal bundles arising via twist
deformation of the structure Hopf algebra of commutative principal bundles have gauge group
isomorphic to the gauge group of the initial classical
bundle.

On one hand in the present paper we improve those results and study the case of principal
bundles with noncommutative base space algebra.
For any Hopf–Galois extension

B=A^{coH}\subseteq A
, with total space algebra
A
, base space
algebra
B
and structure group Hopf algebra
H
, we define the gauge group
\mathrm{Aut}_{B}(A)

of right
H
-comodule algebra morphisms that restrict to the identity on
the subalgebra
B
. This definition encompasses the above mentioned
Drinfeld twist deformations case where the gauge group remains
classical. However, for arbitrary Hopf–Galois extensions it usually gives very
small groups because in general there are few algebra maps on a noncommutative space.
For example on Galois objects (Hopf–Galois extensions with
B

the ground field) the gauge group is just made of characters.

On the other hand we study a second definition of gauge group
for Hopf–Galois extensions with structure Hopf algebra
H
and
equivariant under a triangular Hopf algebra
K
.
As expected, we show that in this case the natural notion of Lie algebra of gauge
transformations is that internal to the representation category of the
triangular Hopf algebra
K
. A Lie algebra object in that category is
a
K
-module with a braided antisymmetric bracket compatible with the

K
-action, that is, it is a
K
-braided Lie algebra. Similarly, the gauge group is a

K
-braided Hopf algebra.
The framework of braided Hopf algebras as Hopf algebras internal in the
representation category of a quasitriangular Hopf algebra has been
proposed in
[
29
,
30
]
. A notion of braided gauge
group was also considered in
[
20
]
, albeit in a different braided
context.

The advantage of considering braided Hopf and Lie algebras is clear
when noncommutative principal bundles, with now noncommutative base
space, are obtained via deformation quantization of classical ones. In this braided approach the (infinite dimensional) vector space underlying infinitesimal gauge
transformations is the same as that of the undeformed bundle.
This follows from the general categorical framework mentioned before, which is well
adapted to Drinfeld twist deformations. It is also illustrated in the key
examples of the instanton bundle and the orthogonal bundle on the
4-sphere
S_{\theta}^{4}
. In this latter both the base space and the structure group
are noncommutative. These results (for the instanton bundle) suggest a
correspondence with those on noncommutative instanton moduli spaces
whose dimensions survives the
\theta
-deformation see for
example
[
28
]
(see also
[
9
,
10
]
).

Another relevant application of this braided gauge Lie algebra approach
is for noncommutative coset spaces –not necessarily related to
twist deformations– whose total space
A
is a triangular Hopf
algebra. The braided Lie algebra of infinitesimal gauge transformations
is the subalgebra of vertical vector fields of the braided Lie algebra
of vector fields. This latter is
generated (over the base space algebra) by the right-invariant vector fields defining the
bicovariant differential calculus à la Woronowicz on
A
. This
relationship between differential calculi and braided infinitesimal
gauge transformations supports their relevance for a theory of connections.

Infinitesimal gauge transformations similar to those in the present
paper have appeared in the
mathematical physics literature. We just mention the recent paper

[
15
]
where only trivial principal bundles are considered using formal
deformation quantization with
\star
-products.
A further independent argument in favour of a theory of noncommutative gauge
groups that, like the braided one we develop, does not drastically depart from the
classical one comes from the Seiberg-Witten map between
commutative and noncommutative gauge theories

[
35
]
. This map
establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the corresponding gauge transformations (yet of a different kind from the
braided gauge transformations we consider) and hence points to
noncommutative gauge equivalence classes that are a deformation of the classical ones.

The
K
-braided Lie algebra of infinitesimal gauge transformations we
consider is an example of Lie algebra in the representation category
of
K
-modules. The first part of the paper provides a self
contained introduction to the theory of braided Hopf algebras, these
are Hopf algebras in the representation category of a
quasitriangular Hopf algebra
K
.
In Section 4 we twist with a Drinfeld 2-cocycle
\mathsf{F}\in K\otimes K
the quasitriangular Hopf algebra

K
to the quasitriangular Hopf algebra

K_{\mathsf{F}}
and recall the monoidal equivalence betweeen
K
-modules and

K_{F}
-modules.

K
-braided Hopf algebras are twisted to

K_{F}
-braided Hopf algebras. In Section 5 we consider
K
triangular, we discuss braided Lie
algebras, that were pioneered by
[
22
]
as generalized Lie
algebras. We twist these braided Lie
algebras and their
universal enveloping algebras, which are braided Hopf algebras. This leads to
isomorphisms (denoted
{\mathcal{D}}
) of
K_{F}
-braided Lie algebras that
we study in detail in particular in the category of relative

K
-modules
B
-bimodules, that is, when there is a
K
-module algebra

B
with a
K
-compatible action on the braided Lie algebra.
Indeed, infinitesimal gauge transformations of a Hopf–Galois extension

B=A^{coH}\subseteq A
form a
K
-module and
a compatible
B
-module: they are a
K
-braided Poisson algebra.

In Section 6 the gauge group of a Hopf–Galois extension
B=A^{coH}\subseteq A
as
H
-equivariant algebra endomorphisms of
A
is
considered, this does not require braided geometry notions. On the contrary, Section 7
applies the braided geometry of the initial sections to study the
braided Lie algebra of infinitesimal gauge transformations of

K
-equivariant Hopf–Galois extensions
B=A^{coH}\subseteq A
. As discussed at the beginning of this introduction,
this notion is natural in the
K
-representation category, and it is
supported by the relationship beween infinitesimal gauge
transformations and differential calculi. The last section applies the Drinfeld twist deformation
machinery to provide
K_{\mathsf{F}}
-braided gauge Lie algebras
from
K
-braided gauge Lie algebras. Thus gauge transformations of
twisted principal bundles arising from commutative principal bundles
are presented. The theory is first illustrated for a trivial principal
bundle. Then the two main examples are those of the instanton and
orthogonal bundles on the
S^{4}_{\theta}
-spheres (
\mathcal{O}(S^{4}_{\theta})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(S^{7}_{\theta})
and
\mathcal{O}(S_{\theta}^{4})\subset\mathcal{O}(SO_{\theta}(5,\mathbb{R}))
respectively)
where the braided Lie algebra of the gauge group is explicitly
presented. Two further examples considering Jordanian twist deformations
(rather than abelian
\theta
-deformations via actions of tori)
are also considered. While all these examples are on the ground field

\mathbb{C}
a last example considers the general case in the
context of formal deformation quantization.

2.
Basic definitions and notations

On this preliminary section we collect basic definitions and fix notation.
All linear spaces are
\mathds{k}
-modules, where

\mathds{k}
is a commutative field with unit
1_{\mathds{k}}
, or the ring of formal power series in a variable
\hbar
over a field.
Much of what follows can be generalised to
\mathds{k}
a commutative unital ring.
We denote the tensor product over
\mathds{k}
by
\otimes
. For
\mathds{k}
-modules
V,W
, we denote by
\tau
the flip operator
\tau:V\otimes W\to W\otimes V,\penalty\ v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v
.

All algebras (coalgebras) are over
\mathds{k}
and assumed to be unital and associative (counital and coassociative). Morphisms of algebras (coalgebras) are unital (counital).
The product in an algebra
A
is denoted
m_{A}:A\otimes A\to A
,
and
the unit map
\eta_{A}:\mathds{k}\to A
, with
1_{A}:=\eta_{A}(1_{\mathds{k}})
the unit element.
The counit and coproduct of a coalgebra
C
are denoted
\varepsilon_{C}:C\to\mathds{k}
and
\Delta_{C}:C\to C\otimes C
respectively.
We use the standard Sweedler notation for the coproduct:
\Delta_{C}(c)={c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}
(with sum understood)
and for its iterations,
\Delta_{C}^{n}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{C})\circ\Delta_{C}^{n-1}:c\mapsto{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{\scriptscriptstyle{(n+1)\;}}
, for all
c\in C
.
Moreover, for a Hopf algebra
H
, we denote
S_{H}:H\to H
its antipode.
We denote by
*
the convolution product in the dual
\mathds{k}
-module

C^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Hom}(C,\mathds{k})
,
(f*g)(c):=f({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})g({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})
, for all
f,g\in C^{\prime}
,
c\in C
.
For a coalgebra
C
, we denote
C^{cop}
the co-opposite coalgebra:
C^{cop}
is the coalgebra structure on the
\mathds{k}
-module
C
with comultiplication
\Delta_{C^{cop}}:=\tau\circ\Delta_{C}

and counit
\varepsilon_{C^{cop}}:=\varepsilon_{C}
. If
C
is a bialgebra, such is
C^{cop}
with multiplication and unit the same of
C
.

2.1.
Hopf algebra modules and comodules

Given a bialgebra (or Hopf algebra)
H
,
a left
H
-module
is a
\mathds{k}
-module
V
with a left

H
-action: a
\mathds{k}
-module map

\vartriangleright_{V}:H\otimes V\to V
,
h\otimes v\mapsto h\vartriangleright_{V}v
, such that

(2.1)

(hk)\vartriangleright_{V}v=h\vartriangleright_{V}(k\vartriangleright_{V}v)\;,\quad 1\vartriangleright_{V}v=v\,,

for all
v\in V
,
h,k\in H
.
Given two
H
-modules
(V,\vartriangleright_{V}),\penalty\ (W,\vartriangleright_{W})
, a map
\psi:V\to W
such that

\psi(h\vartriangleright_{V}v)=h\vartriangleright_{W}(\psi(v))
, for all
h\in H
,
v\in V
,
is called
a morphism of left
H
-modules. This condition will also be referred to as
H
-equivariance.
The tensor product (as
\mathds{k}
-modules)

V\otimes W
is an
H
-module with action

(2.2)

\displaystyle\vartriangleright_{V\otimes W}:H\otimes V\otimes W\longrightarrow V\otimes W\penalty\ ,\quad h\otimes v\otimes w\longmapsto({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{V}v)\otimes({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright_{W}w).

We denote by
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}
the category of left
H
-modules.
It is a monoidal category, whose
unit object is
\mathds{k}
with (trivial) action given by the counit map
\vartriangleright_{\mathds{k}}=\varepsilon_{H}:H\simeq H\otimes\mathds{k}\to\mathds{k}
.
For
H
a Hopf algebra,
the linear space
{\rm{Hom}}(V,W)
of
\mathds{k}
-linear maps
\psi:V\to W
is a left
H
-module with action

\displaystyle\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,W)}:H\otimes{\rm{Hom}}(V,W)

\displaystyle\to{\rm{Hom}}(V,W)

(2.3)

\displaystyle h\otimes\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto h\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,W)}\psi:\;v\mapsto{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{W}\psi(S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright_{V}v).

This action is trivial on the subspace
{\rm{Hom}}_{{}_{H}\mathcal{M}}(V,W)

consisting of
H
-module morphisms:
h\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,W)}\psi=\varepsilon(h)\psi
.

An algebra
A
which is a left
H
-module,
(A,\vartriangleright_{A})
, is called a left

H
-
module algebra
if its multiplication
m_{A}:A\otimes A\to A
and unit
\eta_{A}:\mathds{k}\to A
are morphisms of
H
-modules:

(2.4)

h\vartriangleright_{A}(ab)=({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{A}a)({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright_{A}b)\,,\quad h\vartriangleright_{A}1=\varepsilon(h)1

for all
a,b\in A
,
h\in H
.
We denote by
{}_{H}\mathcal{A}
the category of
left
H
-module algebras, with morphisms in it being
H
-module
morphisms which are also algebra maps.
The algebra
({\rm{Hom}}(V,V),\circ)
of
\mathds{k}
-linear maps from an
H
-module
V
to itself, with multiplication given by map composition and action as in (
2.1
), is a left
H
-module algebra.
A module
V
that is
a left
H
-module and a left
A
-module, with
A
-action denoted

\cdot_{V}
, is called

(H,A)
-
relative Hopf
module
if for all
h\in H
,
a\in A
, and
v\in V
,

(2.5)

h\vartriangleright_{V}(a\cdot_{V}v)=({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{V}a)\cdot_{V}({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright v)\penalty\ .

A morphism of

(H,A)
-relative Hopf modules is a morphism of
right
H
-modules which is also a morphism of left
A
-modules.

Furthermore, a left
H
-
module coalgebra

is a coalgebra
C
which is a left
H
-module and its
coproduct and counit are morphisms of
H
-modules:

(2.6)

{(h\vartriangleright_{C}c)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{(h\vartriangleright_{C}c)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}=({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{C}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\otimes({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright_{C}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\,,\quad\varepsilon(h\vartriangleright_{C}{c})=\varepsilon(h)\varepsilon(c)\,

for all
c\in C
,
h\in H
. We denote by
{}_{H}\mathcal{C}

the category of left
H
-module coalgebras;
morphisms in
{}_{H}{\mathcal{C}}

are
H
-module maps which are also coalgebra maps.

Analogous definitions (and notations) are given for a right
H
-module
(V,\triangleleft_{V})
, and corresponding categories of right
H
-module algebras
\mathcal{A}_{H}
and coalgebras
\mathcal{C}_{H}
. Recall that if
H
is a Hopf algebra, any
left
H
-module
(V,\vartriangleright_{V})
can be made into a right
H
-module
(V,\triangleleft_{V})
with right action

\triangleleft_{V}:V\otimes H\to V,\,\,v\otimes h\mapsto v\triangleleft_{V}h:=S(h)\vartriangleright_{V}v
. Moreover if
(V,\vartriangleright_{V})\in\mathcal{A}_{H}
, then

(V,\triangleleft_{V})\in\mathcal{A}_{H^{cop}}
, with
{H^{cop}}
the co-opposite Hopf algebra.

Dually,
a right
H
-comodule
is a
\mathds{k}
-module
V
with a
\mathds{k}
-linear map

\delta^{V}:V\to V\otimes H
(a right
H
-coaction) such that

(2.7)

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\circ\delta^{V}=(\delta^{V}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\delta^{V}\penalty\ ,\quad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\circ\delta^{V}=\mathrm{id}\,.

In Sweedler notation we write
\delta^{V}:V\to V\otimes H
,

v\mapsto{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}
(with sum understood), and the right
H
-comodule properties
(
2.7
) read

{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{({v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{({v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}={({v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{({v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=:{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\,,\quad{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\,\varepsilon({v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})=v\penalty\

for all
v\in V
.
A morphism between
H
-comodules
V,W
is a
\mathds{k}
-linear map
\psi:V\to W
which is an
H
-comodule map, that is
\delta^{W}\circ\psi=(\psi\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\delta^{V}
, or

{\psi(v)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{\psi(v)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=\psi({v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}

for all
v\in V
.
Also, the tensor product (as
\mathds{k}
-modules)

V\otimes W
is an
H
-comodule with the right
H
-coaction

(2.8)

\displaystyle\delta^{V\otimes W}:V\otimes W\longrightarrow V\otimes W\otimes H\,,\quad v\otimes w

\displaystyle\longmapsto{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{w}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{w}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\penalty\ .

We denote by
\mathcal{M}^{H}
the category of right
H
-comodules; it is a monoidal category. The
unit object is
\mathds{k}
with coaction
\delta^{\mathds{k}}

given by the unit map
\eta_{H}:\mathds{k}\to\mathds{k}\otimes H\simeq H
.

A right
H
-
comodule algebra
is an algebra
A
which is
a right
H
-comodule and has multiplication and unit which are morphisms of
H
-comodules.
This is equivalent to requiring the coaction
\delta^{A}:A\to A\otimes H
to be
a morphism of unital algebras (where
A\otimes H
has the usual tensor
product algebra structure): for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A\,
,

\delta^{A}(a\,a^{\prime})=\delta^{A}(a)\,\delta^{A}(a^{\prime})\,,\quad\delta^{A}(1_{A})=1_{A}\otimes 1_{H}\,.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{H}
the category of right
H
-comodule algebras with
morphisms just
H
-comodule maps which are also algebra maps.

Finally, a right
H
-
comodule coalgebra

is a coalgebra
C
which is a right
H
-comodule and has
coproduct and counit which are morphisms of
H
-comodules,
that is,

(2.9)

{({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}={({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\otimes{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,,\quad\varepsilon({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}){c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=\varepsilon(c)1_{H}\,

for each
c\in C
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{C}}^{H}
the category of right
H
-comodule coalgebras;
its morphisms are
H
-comodule maps which are also coalgebra maps.
Analogous notions and notation can be introduced for left comodules (algebras and coalgebras).

2.2.
Relative modules and K-equivariance

Let
H
be a bialgebra and let
A\in\mathcal{A}^{H}
.
An
(H,A)
-
relative Hopf module

V
is a right
H
-comodule with a left
A
-action
\vartriangleright_{V}
which is a morphism of
H
-comodules: for all
a\in A
and
v\in V
,

(2.10)

{(a\vartriangleright_{V}v)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{(a\vartriangleright_{V}v)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}={a}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\vartriangleright_{V}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\penalty\ .

A morphism of

(H,A)
-relative Hopf modules is a morphism of
right
H
-comodules which is also a morphism of left
A
-modules.
We denote by
{}_{A}\mathcal{M}^{H}
the category of

(H,A)
-relative Hopf modules.
In a similar way one defines the categories of relative Hopf modules
{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}^{\!H}
for
A
acting on the right,
and
{{}_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}^{\!H}\,
for right and left
A

compatible actions.

For two Hopf algebras

K
and
H
, a
K
-equivariant
H
-comodule

V
is
an

(H,K)
-relative Hopf module with
K\in\mathcal{A}^{H}
with a trivial coaction

K\to H\otimes K,k\mapsto k\otimes 1_{H}
. The left
K
-action
\vartriangleright_{V}:K\otimes V\to V
and
the right
H
-coaction
\delta^{V}:V\to V\otimes H
on
V
thus
satisfy
\delta\circ\vartriangleright_{V}=(\vartriangleright_{V}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta)
that is,

(2.11)

{(k\vartriangleright_{V}v)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{(k\vartriangleright_{V}v)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=k\vartriangleright_{V}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}},

for all
k\in K,v\in V
.

We denote by
{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}{}^{H}
the category of
K
-equivariant
H
-comodules.
It is a monoidal category: the tensor product
of
V,W\in{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}{}^{H}
is the object
V\otimes W\in{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}{}^{H}

with tensor
K
-module structure in (
2.2
) and
H
-coaction in (
2.8
).
We also consider the category
{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}{}^{H}

of
K
-equivariant
H
-comodule algebras
, where objects and morphisms are in

{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}{}^{H}
if they are in
{\mathcal{A}}{}^{H}
,
{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}
and
{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}{}^{H}
.

3.
Quasitriangular structures and associated braidings

3.1.
Quasitriangular Hopf algebras

We recall some basic definitions and properties of quasitriangular Hopf algebras referring
to
[
25
, Ch. 8]
or
[
31
, Ch. 2]
for more details and proofs.
This class of algebras is characterized by being
cocommutative up to conjugation by a suitable element.

Definition 3.1
.

A bialgebra
K
, with coproduct
\Delta
, is called
quasitriangular

if there exists an element
\mathsf{R}:=\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\in K\otimes K
(with an implicit sum) which is invertible:

\exists\,{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}\in K\otimes K
, such that
\mathsf{R}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}={\overline{\mathsf{R}}}\mathsf{R}=1\otimes 1
;
\Delta
is quasi-cocommutative with respect to
\mathsf{R}
:

(3.1)

\Delta^{cop}(k)=\mathsf{R}\Delta(k){\overline{\mathsf{R}}}

for each
k\in K
, with
\Delta^{cop}:=\tau\circ\Delta
and
\tau
the flip; and such that

(3.2)

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{23}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{12}.

Here
\mathsf{R}_{12}=\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\otimes 1
and similarly for
\mathsf{R}_{13}
and
\mathsf{R}_{23}
.
Then conditions (
3.2
) become

(3.3)

{\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}=\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\quad;\quad\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}=\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\;.

The element
\mathsf{R}
is called a
universal
R
-matrix
of

K
.
A quasitriangular bialgebra
(K,\mathsf{R})
is
triangular
if
{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}={\mathsf{R}}_{21},
with
{\mathsf{R}}_{21}=\tau(\mathsf{R})=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}
.

The
\mathsf{R}
-matrix of a quasitriangular bialgebra
(K,\mathsf{R})
is unital,

(3.4)

(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathsf{R}=1=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\mathsf{R}

with
\varepsilon
the counit of
K
, and satisfies the quantum Yang–Baxter equation

\mathsf{R}_{12}\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{23}=\mathsf{R}_{23}\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{12},

or in components,

(3.5)

\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\;.

By the defining properties for a
\mathsf{R}
-matrix it follows that if
\mathsf{R}
is a
\mathsf{R}
-matrix of
K
, then

{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{21}
is also a
\mathsf{R}
-matrix of
K
.
Moreover,
K
is cocommutative,
\Delta^{cop}=\Delta
, if and only if
\mathsf{R}=1\otimes 1
is a
\mathsf{R}
-matrix of
K
.

A Hopf algebra which is quasitriangular as a bialgebra is called a
quasitriangular Hopf algebra
.
Its antipode is such that

(3.6)

(S\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathsf{R})={\overline{\mathsf{R}}}\;;\quad(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)({\overline{\mathsf{R}}})=\mathsf{R}\;;\quad(S\otimes S)(\mathsf{R})=\mathsf{R}\;.

and is invertible. Indeed, consider the invertible element

\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}:=S(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha})\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}

with inverse
\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}S^{2}(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha})
. Then,

S^{2}(k)=\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}\,k\,\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}},

for all
k\in K
. This relation and the fact that
S(\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}})\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}=\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}S(\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}})

is central, yields the following expression for the inverse of
S
:

(3.7)

S^{-1}(k)=\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}\,S(k)\,\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}\,,\qquad\forall k\in K\,.

When
(K,\mathsf{R})
is a quasitriangular bialgebra, the monoidal category
{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}
of left

K
-modules is braided monoidal: if
(V,\vartriangleright_{V}),(W,\vartriangleright_{W})
are two left
K
-modules, then the
K
-modules
V\otimes W
and
W\otimes V
, with actions
as in (
2.2
),
are isomorphic via the map

(3.8)

\Psi^{\mathsf{R}}_{V,W}:V\otimes W\longrightarrow W\otimes V\penalty\ ,\quad v\otimes w\longmapsto(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{W}w)\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{V}v)\,.

Remark 3.2
.

In order to show that
the braiding
\Psi^{\mathsf{R}}_{V,W}
is an isomorphism of left
K
-modules, only the condition
{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}\Delta^{cop}=\Delta{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}
in (
3.1
) in Definition
3.1
is needed. Thus, since both
\mathsf{R}
and
{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{21}
are
\mathsf{R}
-matrices for
K
, one could use

{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}
instead of
\mathsf{R}_{21}
in the definition of the braiding.

Additionally, the category of left
K
-module algebras
{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}
is monoidal as well:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(K,\mathsf{R})
be a quasitriangular bialgebra. Let
(A,\vartriangleright_{A}),(C,\vartriangleright_{C})
be left
K
-module algebras,
then the
K
-module
A\otimes C
with tensor product action as in (
2.2
),
is a left
K
-module algebra when endowed with the product

(3.9)

(a\otimes c)\,{\raisebox{1.20554pt}{\rule{2.58334pt}{2.58334pt}}}\,(a^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime}):=a\penalty\ \Psi^{\mathsf{R}}_{C,A}(c\otimes a^{\prime})c^{\prime}=a(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{A}a^{\prime})\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{C}c)c^{\prime}\,.

Moreover, if
\phi:A\to E
and
\psi:C\to F

are morphisms of
K
-module algebras,
then so is
the map
\phi\otimes\psi:A\otimes C\to E\otimes F
,
a\otimes c\mapsto\phi(a)\otimes\psi(c)
,
where
A\otimes C
and
E\otimes F
are both endowed with the

-products.

The
K
-module algebra
(A\otimes C,\,{\raisebox{1.20554pt}{\rule{2.58334pt}{2.58334pt}}}\,)
is the

braided tensor product algebra
of
A
and
C
.
We
denote it by

A\boxtimes C
and write
a\boxtimes c\in A\boxtimes C
for
a\in A
,
c\in C
, and
similarly by

\phi\boxtimes\psi:=\phi\otimes\psi:A\boxtimes C\to E\boxtimes F
the
tensor product of two morphisms.
We denote by
({}_{(K,\mathsf{R})}\mathcal{A},\boxtimes)
or simply
({}_{K}\mathcal{A},\boxtimes)
the monoidal category of left

K
-module algebras with respect to the quasitriangular structure

\mathsf{R}
.

3.2.
Braided Hopf algebras

We can next introduce braided Hopf algebras (see
[
31
, §9.4.2]
or
[
25
, §10.3.3]
):

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(K,\mathsf{R})
be a quasitriangular Hopf algebra.
Let
(L,\vartriangleright_{L})
be both a
K
-module algebra

(L,m_{L},\eta_{L},\vartriangleright_{L})
and a
K
-module coalgebra
(L,\Delta_{L},\varepsilon_{L},\vartriangleright_{L})
. Then
L
is
a
braided bialgebra associated with
(K,\mathsf{R})

if it is a bialgebra
in the braided monoidal category
({}_{K}\mathcal{M},\otimes,\Psi^{\mathsf{R}})
of
K
-modules:

(3.10)

\varepsilon_{L}\circ m_{L}=m_{\mathds{k}}\circ(\varepsilon_{L}\otimes\varepsilon_{L})\,,\quad\Delta_{L}\circ m_{L}=m_{L\boxtimes L}\circ(\Delta_{L}\otimes\Delta_{L})\,.

That is,
\varepsilon_{L}:L\to\mathds{k}
and
\Delta_{L}:L\to L\boxtimes L
are algebra maps
with respect to the product
m_{L}
in
L
and
the product
m_{L\boxtimes L}=(m_{L}\otimes m_{L})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{L}\otimes\Psi^{\mathsf{R}}_{L,L}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{L})
in
L\boxtimes L
(as in
(
3.9
)).
We frequently term
L
a
K
-braided bialgebra omitting to mention the choice
of quasitriangular structure on
K
.

A braided biagebra
L
is a braided Hopf algebra if there is a
K
-module map

S_{L}:L\to L
which is
the convolution inverse of the identity

\mathrm{id}:L\to L
:

(3.11)

m_{L}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{L}\otimes S_{L})\circ\Delta_{L}=\eta_{L}\circ\varepsilon_{L}=m_{L}\circ(S_{L}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{L})\circ\Delta_{L}\penalty\ .

Such a map is called a (braided) antipode.

For later use we recall that the antipode
S_{L}:L\to L
of a braided Hopf algebra
L
is a braided anti-algebra map
and a braided anti-coalgebra map:

(3.12)

S_{L}\circ m_{L}=m_{L}\circ\Psi^{\mathsf{R}}_{L,L}\circ(S_{L}\otimes S_{L})\,,\quad\Delta_{L}\circ S_{L}=(S_{L}\otimes S_{L})\circ\Psi^{\mathsf{R}}_{L,L}\circ\Delta_{L}\,.

Example 3.5
.

For any Hopf algebra
K
, the underlying algebra
(K,m_{K},\eta_{K})
becomes a left
K
-module algebra
with the left adjoint action

(3.13)

\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}:K\otimes K\to K\;,\quad h\otimes k\mapsto h\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}k:={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}kS({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}).

In general, the coproduct
\Delta:K\to K\otimes K
is not a morphism of
K
-modules, for

K\otimes K
carrying the tensor product action as in (
2.2
).
However, if

K
is quasitriangular with
\mathsf{R}
-matrix
\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}
, then the ‘braided’ coproduct

(3.14)

\underline{\Delta}(k):={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}S(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha})\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}S(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}

is now a morphism between the
K
-modules
K
and

K\otimes K
.
This braided coproduct is coassociative and unital; together with the
counit
\varepsilon_{K}
it defines a coalgebra structure on

K
. We use the notation
\underline{\Delta}(k)={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\underline{(1)}}}\otimes{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\underline{(2)}}}
to distinguish it from the coproduct in
K
.
The compatibility with the adjoint
K
-action
implies that

(K,\underline{\Delta},\varepsilon_{K},\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}})
is a
K
-module coalgebra in
{}_{K}\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, the map

(3.15)

\underline{S}:K\to K\;,\quad k\mapsto\underline{S}(k):=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}S(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}k)={\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\alpha}\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}S(k){\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\alpha}

is a left
K
-module morphism and

\underline{K}:=(K,m_{K},\eta_{K},\underline{\Delta},\varepsilon_{K},\underline{S},\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}})

is a braided Hopf algebra associated with the quasitriangular Hopf algebra
(K,\mathsf{R})
.

\blacksquare

In the category of
K
-modules an action of a
K
-module algebra
L

on a
K
-module
M
is an action
\blacktriangleright_{M}:L\otimes M\to M
of the algebra

L
on
M
which is
K
-equivariant:

(3.16)

k\vartriangleright_{M}(\ell\blacktriangleright_{M}m)=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{L}\ell)\blacktriangleright_{M}({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright_{M}m).

Braided Hopf algebras are symmetry objects within the category of
K
-modules.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let (
K,\mathsf{R})
be a quasitriangular Hopf algebra and
L
a
braided bialgebra associated with
K
acting on
K
-modules
M
and

N
as in (
3.16
). There is an action

\blacktriangleright_{M\otimes N}:L\otimes M\otimes N\to M\otimes N\,

on the tensor product
M\otimes N
given by

\ell\blacktriangleright_{M\otimes N}(m\otimes n)={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright_{M}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{M}m)\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright_{N}n\penalty\ .

Proof.

We show that this is an action:
(\ell\ell^{\prime})\blacktriangleright_{M\otimes N}(m\otimes n)=\ell\blacktriangleright_{M\otimes N}\ell^{\prime}\blacktriangleright_{M\otimes N}(m\otimes n)
. For ease of notations in the proof we drop subscripts.

\displaystyle\ell\blacktriangleright(\ell^{\prime}\blacktriangleright

\displaystyle(m\otimes n))=\ell\blacktriangleright({\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright n)

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright({\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m))\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright n)

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright({\mathsf{R}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\blacktriangleright({\mathsf{R}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright n

\displaystyle=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}({\mathsf{R}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}))\blacktriangleright({\mathsf{R}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes((\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright n

\displaystyle=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}({\mathsf{R}_{\beta}}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}))\blacktriangleright({\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes((\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright n

where we have used condition (
3.16
) on
M
for the third equality and the quasitriangular condition (
3.3
) for the last one.
On the other hand

\displaystyle(\ell\ell^{\prime})\blacktriangleright(m\otimes n)

\displaystyle=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright((\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}){\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright n

\displaystyle=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes(({\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})({\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright n

\displaystyle=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes((\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})({\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}\vartriangleright{\ell^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright n

where again we have used the quasitriangular condition for the last equality.

Next, we show
K
-equivariance of
\blacktriangleright_{M\otimes N}
. For all
k\in K,\ell\in L,m\in M,n\in N
,

\displaystyle k\vartriangleright\big(\ell\blacktriangleright(m\otimes n)\big)

\displaystyle=k\vartriangleright\big({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright n\big)

\displaystyle={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\big({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m)\big)\otimes{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright\big((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright n\big)

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\blacktriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright m))\otimes({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\vartriangleright(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}}\vartriangleright n)

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\vartriangleright m))\otimes(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\blacktriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}}\vartriangleright n)

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\ell)\blacktriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright m\otimes{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\vartriangleright n)

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\ell)\blacktriangleright\big({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright(m\otimes n)\big)\penalty\ .

In the second line we used that
L
is a
K
-module algebra, in
the third that the action of
L
on
M
and on
N
is
K
-equivariant.
Then quasi-cocommutativity yields the result.
∎

An action of a braided bialgebra

L
on a
K
-module algebra
A
is a
K
-equivariant action
\blacktriangleright_{A}:L\otimes A\to A

(cf. (
3.16
)) which satisfies the condition

(3.17)

\ell\blacktriangleright_{A}(ac)=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright_{A}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{A}a))\,((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright_{A}c),

for all
a,c\in A
.

An important example of the above construction is given by the adjoint action of a braided Hopf algebra
L
on itself.

Proposition 3.7
.

The
\mathds{k}
-linear map

(3.18)

\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}:L\otimes L\to L,\quad\ell\otimes\ell^{\prime}\mapsto\ell\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}\ell^{\prime}:={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}\ell^{\prime})\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}S_{L}({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}),

is a
K
-equivariant action of
L
on the
K
-module
L
:

\displaystyle(\ell{\ell^{\prime}})\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}x

\displaystyle=\ell\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}({\ell^{\prime}}\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}x)\,

\displaystyle k\vartriangleright_{L}(\ell\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}x)

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright_{L}\ell)\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright_{L}x)

for all
k\in K
and
\ell,\ell^{\prime},x\in L
.

Proof.

To lighten notation we drop all subscripts. The right hand side reads

\displaystyle\ell\blacktriangleright({\ell^{\prime}}\blacktriangleright x)

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright\big({{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\big)\,\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright\big((\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\big)\,\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}{\mathsf{R}^{\beta}}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,(\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}{\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}_{\delta}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\,\mathsf{R}^{\delta}\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\penalty\ ,

were we used the quasitriangular condition in (
3.3
).
The left hand side reads

\displaystyle(\ell{\ell^{\prime}})\blacktriangleright x

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S\big((\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\,{{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\big)

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright\big((\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright S({{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\,\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright S(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\big)

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\ (\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright{{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}{\mathsf{R}_{\delta}}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright S({{\ell^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\,\mathsf{R}^{\delta}{\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}}\vartriangleright S(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\penalty\ ,

where we used the braided algebra map property of the coproduct (
3.10
), the braided
antialgebra map property of the antipode (
3.12
) and again
the quasitriangular condition in (
3.3
).
The left hand side equals the right hand side using the Yang-Baxter
equation and the fact that the antipode
is
K
-equivariant.
We are left to show
K
-equivariance:

\displaystyle k\vartriangleright(\ell\blacktriangleright x)

\displaystyle=k\vartriangleright\big({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\big)

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x))\,({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\vartriangleright(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})))

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\vartriangleright x))\,(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})))

\displaystyle={({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright x))\,(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell})}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\ell)\blacktriangleright({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright x).

In the third line we used quasi-cocommutativity, in the fourth line the
K
-equivariance of the antipode
and that
L
is a
K
-module coalgebra.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

The adjoint action
\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}
in (
3.18
) is an action of
L
on the
K
-module algebra
L

(3.19)

\ell\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}(xy)=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}x))\,((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}y)

for all
\ell,x,y\in L
; it is
henceforth called braided adjoint action.
It satisfies the Jacobi identity (it is a braided action with respect to the nonassociative product (
3.18
)),

(3.20)

\ell\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}(x\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}y)=({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}x))\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}y)\penalty\

for all
\ell,x,y\in L
.

Proof.

We again omit all subscripts.
We start with the braided algebra map property (
3.19
)
(cf.
[
29
, Ex. 2.7]
,
[
30
, App.]
).
The left hand side reads:

\ell\blacktriangleright(xy)={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright(xy))\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright y)\,\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\penalty\ .

It equals the right hand side:

\displaystyle({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright

\displaystyle(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x))\,((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright y)

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\,{(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}})\!\>\!}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright y)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright S(({\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}})\!\>\!}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\mathsf{R}_{\rho}\mathsf{R}_{\delta}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\,(\mathsf{R}^{\rho}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}})\,(\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright y)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright S(\mathsf{R}^{\delta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}}))

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\mathsf{R}_{\delta}\vartriangleright x)\,\big(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright(S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}){\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}})\big)\,(\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright y)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright S(\mathsf{R}^{\delta}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}}))

\displaystyle={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\delta}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\vartriangleright y)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}\mathsf{R}^{\delta}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))

where in the second and third lines we used the quasitriangular condition in (
3.3
).

The proof of property (
3.20
) follows recalling that
\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}
is an action, indeed

\displaystyle({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x))\blacktriangleright((\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\blacktriangleright y)

\displaystyle=\big(({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\blacktriangleright(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x))\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\big)\blacktriangleright y

\displaystyle=\big({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\big)\blacktriangleright y

\displaystyle=\big({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\vartriangleright(S({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}){\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}})\big)\blacktriangleright y

\displaystyle=(\ell x)\blacktriangleright y

\displaystyle=\ell\blacktriangleright(x\blacktriangleright y)

where for the third equality we used again the equality
(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{12}
.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

The map

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}:(L,\vartriangleright_{L})

\displaystyle\to({\rm{Hom}}(L,L),\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(L,L)})

(3.21)

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell},\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}(x):=\ell\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}x={\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{L}S_{L}({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}),\quad x\in L,

is a morphim of
K
-modules.

Proof.

We show that

{\mathcal{L}}_{k\vartriangleright_{L}\ell}=k\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(L,L)}{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}
, for each
k\in K
,
\ell\in L
.
Let
x\in L
, then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k\vartriangleright\ell}(x)

\displaystyle={(k\vartriangleright\ell)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S_{L}({(k\vartriangleright\ell)}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S_{L}({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright x)\,(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright S_{L}({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))

where for the last equality we used that
S_{L}
is a morphism of
H
-modules.
On the other hand, recalling the definition of
\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(L,L)}
from (
2.1
),

\displaystyle(k\vartriangleright{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell})(x)

\displaystyle={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}(S({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright x)

\displaystyle={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\,(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright(S({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright x))\,(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S_{L}({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}S({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}})\vartriangleright x))\,({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright S_{L}({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}))

\displaystyle=({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright{\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\,(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}S({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}})\vartriangleright x))\,(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\vartriangleright S_{L}({\ell}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})),

using the quasi-cocommutativity of
K
for the last equality. Thus, the equality holds.
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
(\underline{K},\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}})
be the braided Hopf algebra associated with
the quasitriangular Hopf algebra

(K,\mathsf{R})
as is Example
3.5
. Then,
the braided adjoint action (
3.18
) coincides with the adjoint action (
3.13
):
\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}=\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}
,
That is for all
h,k\in K
,

{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\underline{(1)}}}(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}k)(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}\underline{S}({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\underline{(2)}}}))={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}kS({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}).

Proof.

From the explicit expressions of the braided coproduct and antipode in
\underline{K}
, we can rewrite the braided adjoint action in the left hand side as

h\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}k={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\nu}S(\mathsf{R}_{\mu}){\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}kS({\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\,{\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\sigma}\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}S(\mathsf{R}^{\mu}{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}^{\nu}){\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\sigma}S({\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}).

By using the quasi triangularity of
K
in (
3.3
), and then the properties (
3.6
) relating
\mathsf{R}
to
{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}
via the altipode, this is written as

\displaystyle{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\nu}S(\mathsf{R}_{\mu})\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\mathsf{R}_{\delta}

\displaystyle kS(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\mathsf{R}_{\beta})\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\sigma}\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}S(\mathsf{R}^{\mu}{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}^{\nu}){\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\sigma}S(\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\mathsf{R}^{\delta})

\displaystyle={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\nu}\mathsf{R}_{\mu}\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\delta}k\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\sigma}\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}S(\mathsf{R}^{\nu})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\mathsf{R}^{\mu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\sigma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\delta}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}

\displaystyle={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\nu}\mathsf{R}_{\mu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\gamma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\delta}k\mathsf{R}_{\beta}S({\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\gamma})S(\mathsf{R}_{\sigma})S(\mathsf{R}^{\nu})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\mathsf{R}^{\mu}\mathsf{R}^{\sigma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\delta}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}.

For the last equality we used

\mathsf{R}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{R}}\mathsf{R}^{\gamma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\sigma}\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{R}}\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\sigma}={\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\gamma}\otimes S({\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\gamma})S(\mathsf{R}_{\sigma})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\sigma}

which is obtained from the expression (
3.7
) for the inverse of the antipode.
Next the quantum Yang-Baxter equation (on the indices
\nu,\mu,\sigma
) gives

{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\mu}\mathsf{R}_{\sigma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\gamma}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\delta}k\mathsf{R}_{\beta}S({\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\gamma})S(\mathsf{R}_{\nu}\mathsf{R}^{\sigma})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\mathsf{R}^{\nu}\mathsf{R}^{\mu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\delta}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}

and we finally obtain

\displaystyle{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}_{\mu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\delta}k\mathsf{R}_{\beta}S(\mathsf{R}_{\nu})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\mathsf{R}^{\nu}\mathsf{R}^{\mu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\delta}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}

\displaystyle={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}k\mathsf{R}_{\beta}S(\mathsf{R}_{\nu})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\mathsf{R}^{\nu}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}

\displaystyle={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}kS(\mathsf{R}_{\nu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}_{\beta})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\mathsf{R}^{\nu}{\overline{\mathsf{R}}}^{\beta}

\displaystyle={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}kS({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})

thus establishing that
h\blacktriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}_{\mathsf{R}}}k=h\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{ad}}k
.
∎

3.3.
Dual structures

For later use we briefly recall the dual notion of
coquasitriangular Hopf algebra and that of
associated braided Hopf algebra, that was used in

[
6
]
.

Definition 3.11
.

A bialgebra
H
is called
coquasitriangular
(or dual
quasitriangular) if it is endowed with a linear form
R:H\otimes H\to\mathds{k}
such that:

(i)
R
is invertible for the convolution product in
H\otimes H
, with inverse denoted
\bar{R}
,

(ii)
m_{op}=R*m*\bar{R}
,

(iii)
R\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id})=R_{13}*R_{23}
and
R\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes m)=R_{13}*R_{12}
,

where
R_{12}=R\otimes\varepsilon:H\otimes H\otimes H\to\mathds{k}
and similarly
for
R_{13}
and
R_{23}
.

The linear form
R
is called a
universal
R
-form
of
H
.
A coquasitriangular bialgebra
(H,R)
is called
cotriangular
if
R=\bar{R}_{21}
.

As for the quasitriangular case,
tensor products of comodule algebras are comodule algebras and
tensor products of comodule algebra maps are again comodule
algebra maps:

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
(H,R)
be a coquasitriangular bialgebra. Let
(A,\delta^{A}),(C,\delta^{C})\in\mathcal{A}^{H}

be right
H
-comodule algebras.
Then the
H
-comodule
A\otimes C
, with tensor product coaction
as in (
2.8
), is a right
H
-comodule algebra when endowed with the product

(3.22)

(a\otimes c)\,{\raisebox{1.20554pt}{\rule{2.58334pt}{2.58334pt}}}\,(a^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime}):=a{a^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\otimes{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}c^{\prime}\penalty\ R_{\!}\left({{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}}\otimes{{a^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}}\right)\,.

Moreover, when
\phi:A\to E
and
\psi:C\to F

are morphisms of
H
-comodule algebras, then so is
the map
\phi\otimes\psi:A\otimes C\to E\otimes F
,
a\otimes c\mapsto\phi(a)\otimes\psi(c)
,
where
A\otimes C
and
E\otimes F
are endowed with the

-products in (
3.22
).

The
H
-comodule algebra
(A\otimes C,\,{\raisebox{1.20554pt}{\rule{2.58334pt}{2.58334pt}}}\,)
is the

braided tensor product algebra
of
A
and
C
. We
denote it by
A\boxtimes C
, and write
a\boxtimes c\in A\boxtimes C
for
a\in A
,
c\in C
and by

\phi\boxtimes\psi:=\phi\otimes\psi:A\boxtimes C\to E\boxtimes F
the
tensor product of two morphisms.
With the tensor product
\boxtimes
the category
(\mathcal{A}^{H},\boxtimes)
of

H
-comodule algebras is a
monoidal category.

The definition of a braided Hopf algebra associated with a
coquasitriangular Hopf algebra
(H,R)
is the same as that in Definition
3.4

for the quasitriangular case, with the braided monoidal category of comodules for
(H,R)
replacing that of modules for
(K,\mathsf{R})
.

In particular for any Hopf algebra
H
, the data
(H,\Delta_{H},\varepsilon_{H},\mathrm{Ad})
is an
H
-comodule coalgebra, with the right adjoint coaction

\mathrm{Ad}:H\to H\otimes H,\penalty\ h\mapsto{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\otimes S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}){h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}
.
When
(H,R)
is coquasitriangular
\mathrm{Ad}
is an algebra map for the
‘braided’ product in
H
given by

(3.23)

h{\>\cdot_{{}^{{}_{{{\>\!\!\!\!\!-}}}}}}k:={h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}R\big(S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}){h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}\otimes{S({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})}\big)\;,\quad h,k\in H\,.

Then
{\underline{H}}:=(H,{\>\cdot_{{}^{{}_{{{\>\!\!\!\!\!-}}}}}},\eta_{H},\Delta_{H},\varepsilon_{H},\mathrm{Ad})
is a
braided bialgebra associated with
(H,R)
. Furthermore
{\underline{H}}

is a braided Hopf algebra with antipode
\underline{S}
defined by

(3.24)

\underline{S}(h):=S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})R\big({S^{2}({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}})S({h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})}\otimes{{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(4)}}})\;,\quad h\in H\,.

4.
Twisting braided Hopf algebras

We recall some basic definitions and properties of the theory of
Drinfel’d twists
[
17
,
18
]
, see also
[
19
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
K
be a bialgebra (or Hopf algebra). A
twist
for
K
is an invertible element

\mathsf{F}\in K\otimes K
which is unital,

(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathsf{F})=1=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)(\mathsf{F})
,
and satisfies

(4.1)

(\mathsf{F}\otimes 1)[(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathsf{F})]=(1\otimes\mathsf{F})[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(\mathsf{F})]\penalty\ ,

referred to as the twist condition.

If
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
denotes the inverse of the twist,
\mathsf{F}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}=1\otimes 1={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}\mathsf{F}
,
the condition (
4.1
) can be written equivalently as

(4.2)

[(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})({\overline{\mathsf{F}}})]({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}\otimes 1)=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)({\overline{\mathsf{F}}})](1\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{F}}})\penalty\ .

We use the notation
\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}
and
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}=:{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}
with an implicit summation understood, for the twist and its inverse.
The identities (
4.1
), (
4.2
), are then rewritten respectively as

(4.3)

\mathsf{F}^{\beta}{\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes\mathsf{F}_{\beta}{\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\otimes\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{F}^{\beta}{\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes\mathsf{F}_{\beta}{\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}

(4.4)

{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\otimes{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\otimes{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\otimes{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}\;.

Remark 4.2
.

When
K
is not finite
dimensional over
\mathds{k}
, the twist
\mathsf{F}
may not necessarily be an
element of
K\otimes K
, but rather it belongs to a topological
completion of the tensor product algebra. In the
examples of this paper we avoid this problem either by diagonalizing

\mathsf{F}
or by considering representations of
\mathsf{F}
involving only a
finite number of addends in
\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\otimes F_{\alpha}
.

Example 4.3
.

The
\mathsf{R}
-matrix
\mathsf{R}
of a quasitriangular bialgebra
K
is a twist for
K
. Condition (
4.1
) follows from the quasitriangular condition (
3.3
) and the quantum Yang-Baxter equation:

(\mathsf{R}\otimes 1)[(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathsf{R}]=\mathsf{R}_{12}\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{23}=\mathsf{R}_{23}\mathsf{R}_{13}\mathsf{R}_{12}=(1\otimes\mathsf{R})[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)
.

\blacksquare

When
K
has a twist,
K
can be endowed with a second bialgebra structure which is obtained by deforming its coproduct and leaving its counit and multiplication unchanged:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}
be a twist on a bialgebra
(K,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon)
. Then
the algebra
(K,m,\eta)
with coproduct

(4.5)

\Delta_{\mathsf{F}}(k):=\mathsf{F}\Delta(k){\overline{\mathsf{F}}}=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\otimes\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}\penalty\ ,\qquad k\in K

and counit
\varepsilon
is a bialgebra. If in addition
K
is a Hopf algebra, then the
twisted bialgebra
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}:=(K,m,\eta,\Delta_{\mathsf{F}},\varepsilon)

is a Hopf algebra with antipode

S_{\mathsf{F}}(k):=\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}S(k)\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}},

where
\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}
is the invertible element

\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}:=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}S(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha})
and
\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}=S({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}){\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}
its inverse.

We use the notation
\Delta_{\mathsf{F}}(k)=:{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[1]}}\otimes{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[2]}}
for the coproduct in
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
to distinguish it from the original coproduct
\Delta(k)={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}
in
K
.

Let us recall that the inverse
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
of the twist
\mathsf{F}
is a twist for the twisted bialgebra
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
, with the twist condition for
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}\in{K_{\mathsf{F}}}\otimes{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
coinciding with (
4.4
). Clearly, using
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
to deform the coproduct in
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
leads back to the initial bialgebra
({K_{\mathsf{F}}})_{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}}=K
.

For later use, we next recall some results which are dual to those related to deformations of bialgebras via 2-cocycles
[
16
]
, and which were addressed in
[
6
, §4]
.

Let
K
be a bialgebra (or Hopf algebra) with a twist
\mathsf{F}\in K\otimes K
and
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
the resulting twisted
bialgebra, as above.
Any
K
-module
V
with left action
\vartriangleright_{V}:K\otimes V\to V
, is also a
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-module
with the same linear map
\vartriangleright_{V}
, now thought as a map

\vartriangleright_{V}:{K_{\mathsf{F}}}\otimes V\to V
.
Indeed, the module conditions in (
2.1
)
only involve the algebra structure of
K
, and the twisted bialgebra

{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
coincides with
K
as algebra.
When thinking of
V
as a
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-module we denote it by
V_{\mathsf{F}}
, with action
\vartriangleright_{V_{F}}
.
Moreover, any
K
-module morphism
\psi:V\to W

can be thought as a morphism
\psi_{F}:V_{\mathsf{F}}\to W_{\mathsf{F}}

since, as stated, the action of
K
on any
V,W
coincides with the action of
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
on
V_{\mathsf{F}},W_{\mathsf{F}}
.
This amounts to say that there is an equivalence of category

\Gamma:{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}\to{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{M}}
with the functor
\Gamma
just the identity both on objects and on morphisms:

\Gamma(V):=V_{\mathsf{F}}
and
\Gamma(\psi):=\psi_{F}
. The use of
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
as a twist for
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
, which ‘twists back’
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
to
K
, inverts the construction going from
{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{M}}
to
{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}
.

With the monoidal structure one needs the deformed coproduct (
4.5
).
Explicitly, for
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
modules
(V_{\mathsf{F}},\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}})
and
(W_{\mathsf{F}},\vartriangleright_{W_{\mathsf{F}}})
,
the tensor product action of
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
on
V_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}W_{\mathsf{F}}\simeq V\otimes W
(as linear spaces)
is given by

(4.6)

\displaystyle\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}W_{\mathsf{F}}}:{K_{\mathsf{F}}}\otimes(V_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}W_{\mathsf{F}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow V_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}W_{\mathsf{F}}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle k\otimes v\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}w

\displaystyle\longmapsto({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[1]}}\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}v)\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[2]}}\vartriangleright_{W_{\mathsf{F}}}w)\penalty\ .

We denote by
({}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{M}},\otimes_{\mathsf{F}})
the monoidal category of left
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-modules. We have then:

Proposition 4.5
.

The functor
\Gamma:{}_{K}{\mathcal{M}}\to{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{M}}
together with the natural isomorphism
\varphi:\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}\circ(\Gamma\times\Gamma)\Rightarrow\Gamma\circ\otimes
given by the isomorphism of
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-modules

(4.7)

\displaystyle\varphi_{V,W}:V_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}W_{\mathsf{F}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(V\otimes W)_{\mathsf{F}}

\displaystyle v\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}w

\displaystyle\longmapsto({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}v)\otimes({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{W_{\mathsf{F}}}w)\,,

is an equivalence of monoidal categories
({}_{K}{\mathcal{M}},\otimes)
and

({}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{M}},\otimes_{\mathsf{F}})
.

As a consequence of the twist condition (
4.4
) the isomorphisms
\varphi_{-,-}
satisfy

(4.8)

\varphi_{V,W\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}Z}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{V_{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}\varphi_{W,Z})=\varphi_{V\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}W,Z}\circ(\varphi_{V,W}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}\mathrm{id}_{Z_{F}})

for every
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-modules
V_{\mathsf{F}},W_{\mathsf{F}},Z_{\mathsf{F}}
.

There is also an equivalence between the category
{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}
of left
K
-module algebras
and the corresponding category
{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{A}}
of
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-module algebras

(4.9)

\Gamma:{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}\to{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{A}}\,,\quad{(A,m_{A},\eta_{A},\vartriangleright_{A})\mapsto(A_{\mathsf{F}},m_{A_{\mathsf{F}}},\eta_{A_{\mathsf{F}}},\vartriangleright_{A_{\mathsf{F}}})}\;.

It is no longer the identity on objects.
Given
A\in{}_{K}{\mathcal{A}}
with multiplication
m_{A}
and unit
\eta_{A}
, in order for the action
\vartriangleright_{A_{\mathsf{F}}}
to be an algebra map one has to define a new product on the
K
-module

A_{\mathsf{F}}=\Gamma(A)
. The new algebra
structure
m_{A_{\mathsf{F}}},\eta_{A_{\mathsf{F}}}
on

A_{\mathsf{F}}\in{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{A}}
is defined by using the natural
isomorphisms
\varphi
in (
4.7
):

m_{A_{\mathsf{F}}}:=\Gamma(m_{A})\circ\varphi_{A,A}\,,\quad\eta_{A_{\mathsf{F}}}:=\Gamma(\eta_{A})

Explicitly, the unit is unchanged, while the product is deformed to

\displaystyle m_{A_{\mathsf{F}}}:A_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}A_{\mathsf{F}}

\displaystyle\,\longrightarrow A_{\mathsf{F}}

(4.10)

\displaystyle a\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}a^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\longmapsto a{\,\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.55}[0.55]{\mbox{$\bullet_{\mbox{$\mathsf{F}\,$}}$}}}}a^{\prime}:=({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{A}a)\,({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{A}a^{\prime})

The functor
\Gamma
is the identity on morphism:
for any algebra map
\psi:A\to A^{\prime}
one shows that

\Gamma(\psi)=\psi_{F}:A_{\mathsf{F}}\to A^{\prime}_{\mathsf{F}}
is an algebra map for
the deformed products.

Similarly to above, there is also an equivalence between the category

{}_{K}{\mathcal{C}}
of left
K
-module coalgebras and that of
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-module coalgebras

(4.11)

\Gamma:{}_{K}{\mathcal{C}}\to{}_{{K_{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathcal{C}}\,,\quad{(C,\Delta_{C},\varepsilon_{C},\vartriangleright_{C})\mapsto(C_{\mathsf{F}},\Delta_{C_{\mathsf{F}}},\varepsilon_{C_{\mathsf{F}}},\vartriangleright_{C_{\mathsf{F}}})}\;.

Again,
\Gamma
acts as the identity on morphisms, but not on objects.
Each
K
-module coalgebra
C
with costructures
\Delta_{C},\varepsilon_{C}

is mapped to the
{K_{\mathsf{F}}}
-module coalgebra
C_{\mathsf{F}}=\Gamma(C)
with costructures

\Delta_{C_{\mathsf{F}}},\varepsilon_{C_{\mathsf{F}}}

defined by

\Delta_{C_{\mathsf{F}}}:=\varphi_{C,C}^{-1}\circ\Gamma(\Delta_{C})\,,\quad\varepsilon_{C_{\mathsf{F}}}:=\Gamma(\varepsilon_{C}).

Explicitly, while the counit does not change, the
deformed coproduct is

(4.12)

\Delta_{C_{\mathsf{F}}}:C_{\mathsf{F}}\longrightarrow C_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes_{\mathsf{F}}C_{\mathsf{F}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ c\longmapsto{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[1]}}\otimes{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[2]}}:=(\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{C}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}})\otimes(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{C}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\,.

4.1.
K
-modules of linear maps

We study twist deformations of linear maps of
K
-modules
for later applications in §
5.5
to braided derivations.

Consider the
K
-module algebra

({\rm{Hom}}(V,V),\circ)
of linear maps of a
K
-module
V
, with action
\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,V)}
as in (
2.1
).
On the one hand, we obtain a
K_{\mathsf{F}}
-module algebra
({\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V),\circ_{\mathsf{F}})
out of
({\rm{Hom}}(V,V),\circ)
by changing the multiplication

\circ
as in (
4
):

(4.13)

\psi\circ_{\mathsf{F}}\phi=({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,V)}\psi)\circ({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,V)}\phi)\,.

The
K_{\mathsf{F}}
-action
\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V)}

coincides with
\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V,V)}
as linear map.
On the other hand, there is the
K_{\mathsf{F}}
-module algebra
({\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}}),\circ)
of linear maps of
the
K_{\mathsf{F}}
-module
V_{\mathsf{F}}
with action

\displaystyle\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})}:K_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})

\displaystyle\to{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})

(4.14)

\displaystyle k\otimes\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto k\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})}\psi:\;a\mapsto{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[1]}}\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}\psi(S_{\mathsf{F}}({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[2]}})\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}a).

These two module algebras are isomorphic (cf.
[
4
, Th. 4.7]
):

Proposition 4.6
.

The

K_{\mathsf{F}}
-module algebras
({\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V),\circ_{\mathsf{F}})
and
({\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}}),\circ)
are isomorphic via the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V)

\displaystyle\to{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})

(4.15)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{D}(\psi):v\mapsto({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V)}\psi)({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{V}v)

with
inverse

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{-1}:{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})

\displaystyle\to{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V)

(4.16)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\psi):v\mapsto(\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})}\psi)(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}v)\;.

Proof.

We first observe that from the twist condition and the definition of the action on endomorphims, we can also write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\psi)(v)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{V}\psi(S(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\vartriangleright_{V}v)\;;\;

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\psi)(v)

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}\psi(S_{\mathsf{F}}({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha})\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}v).

Then it is easy to see that the two maps are one the inverse of the other (also recalling that the actions
\vartriangleright_{V}
and
\vartriangleright_{V_{\mathsf{F}}}
coincides as linear maps).
We show the
K_{\mathsf{F}}
-equivariance
k\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}(V_{\mathsf{F}},V_{\mathsf{F}})}\mathcal{D}(\psi)=\mathcal{D}(k\vartriangleright_{{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathsf{F}}(V,V)}\psi)
:

\displaystyle(k\vartriangleright\mathcal{D}(\psi))(v)

\displaystyle={k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[1]}}\vartriangleright\mathcal{D}(\psi)(S_{\mathsf{F}}({k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{[2]}})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\vartriangleright\mathcal{D}(\psi)(\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}S(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright\psi(S(\mathsf{F}_{\gamma})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}S(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\psi(S(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha}{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{F}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright\big((k\vartriangleright\psi)(S(\mathsf{F}_{\alpha})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\vartriangleright v)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(k\vartriangleright\psi)(v)\;.

Next we show that
\mathcal{D}(\psi\circ_{\mathsf{F}}\phi)=\mathcal{D}(\psi)\circ\mathcal{D}(\phi)
.
For that we compute

\displaystyle(\psi\circ_{\mathsf{F}}\phi)(v)

\displaystyle=\big(({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright\psi)\circ({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\vartriangleright\phi)\big)(v)

\displaystyle=({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright\psi)({{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\phi(S({{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle={{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\psi(S({{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}){{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\phi(S({{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright\psi(S({{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\mathsf{F}_{\gamma}){{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\phi(S({{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(3)}}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright\psi(S(\mathsf{F}_{\gamma})S({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}){{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\vartriangleright\phi(S({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}\vartriangleright\psi(S(\mathsf{F}_{\gamma})\bar{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{F}^{\beta}\vartriangleright\phi(S({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\mathsf{F}_{\beta})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright\mathcal{D}(\psi)(\mathsf{F}^{\beta}\vartriangleright\phi(S(\mathsf{F}_{\beta})S({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha})\vartriangleright v)

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\vartriangleright\mathcal{D}(\psi)\big(\mathcal{D}(\phi)(\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}}S({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha})\vartriangleright v)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mathcal{D}(\psi)\circ\mathcal{D}(\phi))(v)

thus showing that
\mathcal{D}
is an algebra morphism.
∎

4.2.
Quasitriangular bialgebras

We consider the case when the twist comes from a
quasitriangular bialgebra
(K,\mathsf{R})
. The twisted bialgebra
K_{F}
is still quasitriangular. Moreover, the isomorphisms
\varphi

in (
4.7
) for module algebras are algebra maps (Proposition
4.10
); for this we need the following result.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
(K,\mathsf{R})
be a quasitriangular bialgebra and
\mathsf{F}\in K\otimes K
a twist for
K
.
Then the following identity holds in
K^{\otimes 4}
:

(4.17)

[(\Delta\otimes\Delta)\mathsf{F}](1\otimes\mathsf{R}_{21}\otimes 1)[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\tau\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta\otimes\Delta){\overline{\mathsf{F}}}]\\
=({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{F}}})(1\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{F}}}_{21}\otimes 1)[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\tau\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathsf{F}\otimes\mathsf{F})]\;,

where
{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{F}}}_{21}=\mathsf{F}\,\mathsf{R}_{21}\,{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{21}
. That is,

(4.18)

{\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\otimes{\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\boxtimes_{\mathsf{F}}{\mathsf{F}_{\gamma}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\mathsf{R}^{\beta}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\otimes{\mathsf{F}_{\gamma}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}{{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}\\
={\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\beta}\mathsf{F}^{\gamma}\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\beta}{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\mathsf{F}^{\delta}\boxtimes_{\mathsf{F}}{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}^{\mu}{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{F}}}^{\alpha}\mathsf{F}_{\gamma}\otimes{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}_{\mu}\mathsf{F}_{\delta}\,.

Proof.

Firstly we observe that the twist condition (
4.1
) for
\mathsf{F}
gives

[(\Delta\otimes\Delta)\mathsf{F}]=({\overline{\mathsf{F}}}\otimes 1\otimes 1)[(1\otimes(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathsf{F}][(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta^{2})\mathsf{F}]

and, similarly, the twist condition (
4.2
) written for the inverse
{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
of
